Stable Canonical Rules and Formulas for Pre-transitive Logics via Definable Filtration
Tenyo Takahashi

Abstract
We generalize the theory of stable canonical rules by adopting definable filtration, a generalization of the method of filtration. We show that for a modal rule system or a modal logic that admits definable filtration, each extension is axiomatizable by stable canonical rules. Moreover, we provide an algebraic presentation of Gabbay’s filtration and generalize stable canonical formulas and the axiomatization results via stable canonical formulas for 𝖪𝟦 \mathsf{K4} to pre-transitive logics 𝖪𝟦 𝟣 𝗆 + 𝟣 = 𝖪 + m + 1 ​ p → ​ p \mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}=\mathsf{K}+\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox Finally, we introduce m m -stable canonical formulas, strengthening the axiomatization results for these logics. 1 1 1 This paper is based on [ 30 , Chapter 3] .



1
Introduction

One of the most powerful tools in the study of the lattice of modal logics and superintuitionistic logics is
characteristic formulas
. Characteristic formulas, also called
algebra-based formulas
or
frame-based formulas
, refer to variations of formulas that are defined from finite algebras or finite relational structures (e.g., finite Kripke frames) so that their validity has a semantic characterization. Thus, logics axiomatized by characteristic formulas are often easier to analyze. We refer to
[
5
]
for a comprehensive overview of the techniques of characterization formulas for superintuitionistic logics. The first type of characteristic formulas,
Jankov formulas
or
Jankov-de Jongh formulas
, was introduced and studied by Jankov
[
21
]
and independently by de Jongh
[
23
]
for superintuitionistic logics. Jankov formulas were used in
[
21
]
to construct continuum many superintuitionistic logics. Their modal logic analog,
Fine formulas
or
Jankov-Fine formulas
, was introduced by Fine
[
17
]
. A generalization to
n
-transitive modal logics was constructed by Rautenberg
[
28
]
using algebraic methods. Further development includes
subframe formulas
,
cofinal subframe formulas
, and
canonical formulas
(see, e.g.,
[
16
, Chapter 9]
). A remarkable feature of canonical formulas is that they axiomatize all transitive logics
[
32
]
, which makes them particularly useful. For various applications of the technique of canonical formulas, see
[
16
, Chapters 9-16]
.

Recently, Jeřábek
[
22
]
generalized the idea of canonical formulas to inference rules and defined
canonical rules
. These rules are based on a version of
selective filtration
(see, e.g.,
[
16
, Section 9.3]
) and axiomatize all rule systems over
\mathsf{K4}
. Jeřábek
[
22
]
applied canonical rules to construct admissible bases and to provide an alternative proof of the decidability of the admissibility problem for transitive logics (such as
\mathsf{K4}
,
\mathsf{S4}
, and
\mathsf{GL}
) as well as the intuitionistic logic.

Bezhanishvili et al.
[
7
]
, motivated by the method of
filtration
(see, e.g.,
[
14
, Section 2.3]
and
[
16
, Section 5.3]
), defined
stable canonical rules
and showed that they axiomatize all rule systems over the least modal rule system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
. They also introduced
stable canonical formulas
for
\mathsf{K4}
as an alternative to canonical formulas. For various applications of stable canonical rules and formulas, see
[
13
,
12
,
8
,
11
]
. While selective filtration is mostly used for transitive logics such as
\mathsf{K4}
and
\mathsf{S4}
, standard filtration is also constructed for non-transitive logics such as
\mathsf{K}
. So, it is anticipated that stable canonical rules and formulas can also be useful for studying various properties of non-transitive logics.

In this paper, we generalize the theory of stable canonical rules and formulas by adopting
definable filtration
, a generalization of standard filtration introduced in
[
24
]
. Recall that the method of filtration uses a finite subformula-closed set
\Theta
to transform a Kripke model into a finite one, by identifying points that agree on all formulas in
\Theta
and imposing requirements on the accessibility relation according to
\Theta
. Definable filtration, on the other hand, can use a larger set
\Theta^{\prime}\supseteq\Theta
to identify points so that the equivalence relation is finer, while the requirements on the accessibility relation are still determined by
\Theta
. So, filtration is a special type of definable filtration where
\Theta^{\prime}=\Theta
. A typical example of definable filtration is Gabbay’s filtration
[
18
]
for the pre-transitive logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}=\mathsf{K}+\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{m+1}p\to\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp

(m\geq 1)
, which we will discuss in
Section

4
.

We generalize the axiomatization results via stable canonical rules by allowing the base rule system or logic to be any one that admits definable filtration. A related notion motivated by filtration is
\mathsf{M}
-stable logics
, which have the finite model property (the fmp) if the base logic
\mathsf{M}
admits filtration
[
8
]
. We observe that the assumption of admitting filtration can be weakened to admitting definable filtration. For example, the logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
admit definable filtration, but as far as we know, there is no known filtration for these logics. Moreover, we provide an algebraic presentation of Gabbay’s filtration and generalize stable canonical formulas and the axiomatization result for
\mathsf{K4}
to the logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
. As consequences, we obtain the fmp of
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-stable logics and a characterization of splitting and union-splitting logics in the lattice
\mathsf{NExt}{\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}}
. These generalizations enable a fully semantic investigation of rule systems and logics extending one that admits definable filtration, based on which a combinatorial method is introduced in
[
30
, Chapter 4]
to obtain the fmp for large classes of logics and rule systems, including
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-stable logics. Finally, through our proof, we observe that the axiomatization result can be further strengthened by considering a stronger type of formulas, which we call
m
-stable canonical formulas
.

This paper is organized as follows.
Section

2
reviews the necessary preliminaries, including stable canonical rules.
Section

3
discusses definable filtrations from an algebraic perspective and their connection to the theory of stable canonical rules.
Section

4
presents an algebraic formulation of Gabbay’s filtration for pre-transitive logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
. Based on this,
Section

5
develops the theory of stable canonical formulas for
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
.
Section

6
defines
m
-stable canonical formulas and shows the axiomatization result via them. Finally,
Section

7
outlines potential directions for future work.

2
Preliminaries

We assume familiarity with modal logic. We refer to
[
14
,
16
]
for basic syntax and Kripke semantics. We will only work with normal modal logics in this paper, so we simply call them logic. For a logic
L
,
\mathsf{NExt}{L}
denotes the lattice of normal extensions of
L
. We will use

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptb
. Valuations, satisfaction, and validity are defined as usual. The above abbreviation applies to operations on modal algebras as well. Recall that a
modal space
is a pair
\mathfrak{X}=(X,R)
of a Stone space and a binary relation
R\subseteq X\times X
such that
R[x]
is closed for each
x\in X
and the set
R^{-1}[U]
is clopen for each clopen set
U\subseteq X
. Modal spaces are also known as
descriptive frames
. Finite modal spaces are identified with finite Kripke frames. Continuous p-morphisms between modal spaces are simply called p-morphisms. It is well-known that the category of modal algebras and homomorphisms is dually equivalent to the category of modal spaces and p-morphisms.

We sketch the construction of the dual equivalence functors, which is built on top of
Stone duality
(see, e.g.,
[
14
, Section 5.4]
). Given a modal algebra
\mathfrak{A}
, the dual modal space of
\mathfrak{A}
is
\mathfrak{A}_{*}=(A_{*},R_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to4.62pt{\vbox to4.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.31197pt\lower-2.31197pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -2.95 M -0.03 -2.92 L -2.92 -0.03 C -2.94 -0.01 -2.94 0.01 -2.92 0.03 L -0.03 2.92 C -0.01 2.94 0.01 2.94 0.03 2.92 L 2.92 0.03 C 2.94 0.01 2.94 -0.01 2.92 -0.03 L 0.03 -2.92 C 0.01 -2.94 -0.01 -2.94 -0.03 -2.92 Z M 0 2.95}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt})
where

1.

A_{*}
is the dual Stone space of

:

as a set is the set of all ultrafilters on

, and the topology is generated by the base

,

is defined by

Given a homomorphism
h:\mathfrak{A}\to\mathfrak{B}
, the dual p-morphism of
h
is
h_{*}:\mathfrak{B}_{*}\to\mathfrak{A}_{*};x\mapsto h^{-1}[x]
. Conversely, given a modal space
\mathfrak{X}=(X,R)
, the dual modal algebra of
\mathfrak{X}
is
\mathfrak{X}^{*}=(X^{*},\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

where

1.

,

2.

for

.

Given a p-morphism
f:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
, the dual homomorphism is
f^{*}:\mathfrak{Y}^{*}\to\mathfrak{X}^{*};a\to f^{-1}[a]
.

We assume familiarity with basic universal algebra and refer to
[
2
,
15
]
for an introduction. For a class
\mathcal{K}
of modal algebras,
\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{K})
(resp.
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{K})
) denotes the least variety (resp. universal class) containing
\mathcal{K}
. The following characterization of subdirectly irreducible (s.i. for short) modal algebras is from Rautenberg
[
28
]
. Note that an opremum may not be unique.

Proposition 2.1
.

A modal algebra
\mathfrak{A}
is s.i. iff
\mathfrak{A}
has an
opremum
, that is, an element
c\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
c\neq 1
and for any
a\neq 1
, there is
n\in\omega
such that
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{\leq n}a\leq c
.

Stable homomorphisms and the closed domain condition

Stable homomorphisms and the closed domain condition for modal algebras and modal spaces were introduced in
[
7
]
, generalizing that for Heyting algebras and Priestley spaces introduced in
[
6
]
. Intuitively, a stable homomorphism
h
does not preserve
(so it is not a modal algebra homomorphism), but it does satisfy the inequality
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}

holds. Stable homomorphisms were studied in

as semihomomorphisms and in

as continuous morphisms.

Definition 2.2
.

Let

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pth(a)\leq h(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

for all

.

2.

Let

, we say that

if

. For

, we say that

if

.

These definitions dualize to modal spaces as follows.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}=(X,R)
and
\mathfrak{Y}=(Y,Q)
be modal spaces.

1.

A continuous map
f:X\to Y
is a
stable map
if
xRy
implies
f(x)Qf(y)
for all
x,y\in X
.

2.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a stable map. For a clopen subset
D\subseteq Y
, we say that
f
satisfies the
closed domain condition (CDC) for
D
if

Q[f(x)]\cap D\neq\emptyset\Rightarrow f(R[x])\cap D\neq\emptyset.

For a set
\mathcal{D}
of clopen subsets of
Y
, we say that
f:X\to Y
satisfies
the closed domain condition (CDC) for
\mathcal{D}
if
f
satisfies CDC for all
D\in\mathcal{D}
.

It follows directly from the definition that a stable homomorphism
h:A\to B
satisfying CDC for
A
is a modal algebra homomorphism, and a stable map
f:X\to Y
satisfying CDC for
\mathcal{P}(Y)
is a p-morphism.

Notation 2.4
.

We write
h:\mathfrak{A}\hookrightarrow_{D}\mathfrak{B}
if
h
is a stable embedding satisfying CDC for
D
, and
\mathfrak{A}\hookrightarrow_{D}\mathfrak{B}
if there is such an
h
. We write
f:\mathfrak{X}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{D}}\mathfrak{Y}
if
f
is a surjective stable map satisfying CDC for
\mathcal{D}
, and
\mathfrak{X}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{D}}\mathfrak{Y}
if there is such an
f
.

Stable canonical rules

We refer to
[
7
,
22
,
25
]
for inference rules and consequence relations in modal logic. A
modal multi-conclusion rule

\rho
is an expression of the form

\Gamma

\Delta

(or
\Gamma/\Delta
) where
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite sets of formulas. If
\Delta
is a singleton,
\rho
is called
single-conclusion
. If
\Gamma=\emptyset
,
\rho
is called
assumption-free
. We will only address modal multi-conclusion rules, so we simply call them
rules
. A single-conclusion assumption-free rule
/\varphi
can be identified with the formula
\varphi
.

A
normal modal multi-conclusion consequence relation
or
normal modal multi-conclusion rule system
is a set
\mathcal{S}
of rules satisfying:

1.

\varphi/\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
,

2.

\varphi,\varphi\to\psi/\psi\in\mathcal{S}
,

3.

\varphi/\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
,

4.

/\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
for all
\varphi\in\mathsf{K}
,

5.

\Gamma/\Delta\in\mathcal{S}
implies
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}/\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
(
weakening
),

6.

\Gamma/\Delta,\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
and
\Gamma,\varphi/\Delta\in\mathcal{S}
implies
\Gamma/\Delta\in\mathcal{S}
(
cut
),

7.

\Gamma/\Delta\in\mathcal{S}
implies
\sigma(\Gamma)/\sigma(\Delta)\in\mathcal{S}
for any substitution
\sigma
(
substitution
).

We simply call normal modal multi-conclusion consequence relations
rule systems
. For a set
\mathcal{R}
of rules, let
\mathcal{S}_{0}+\mathcal{R}
be the least rule system containing
\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{R}
. For a logic
L
, let
\mathcal{S}_{L}
be the least rule system containing
L
. When
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{0}+\mathcal{R}
, we say that
\mathcal{S}
is
axiomatized
by
\mathcal{R}
over
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. When
\mathcal{S}_{L}
is axiomatized by
\mathcal{R}
over
\mathcal{S}_{L_{0}}
, we say that
L
is axiomatized by
\mathcal{R}
over
L_{0}
. When
\mathcal{S}+\rho=\mathcal{S}+\rho^{\prime}
, we say that
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
are
equivalent
over
\mathcal{S}
.

A modal algebra
\mathfrak{A}

validates
a rule
\Gamma/\Delta
, written
\mathfrak{A}\models\Gamma/\Delta
, if for any valuation
V
on
\mathfrak{A}
,
V(\gamma)=1
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
implies
V(\delta)=1
for some
\delta\in\Delta
. Recall that logics correspond one-to-one to varieties and we write
\mathcal{V}(L)\coloneq\{\mathfrak{A}:\mathfrak{A}\models L\}
for a logic
L
and
\mathsf{Log}(\mathcal{V})\coloneq\{\varphi:\mathcal{V}\models\varphi\}
for a variety
\mathcal{V}
. In a similar manner, each rule corresponds to a universal sentence and each rule system to a universal class. We write
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{S})\coloneq\{\mathfrak{A}:\mathfrak{A}\models\mathcal{S}\}
for a rule system
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{U})\coloneq\{\rho:\mathcal{U}\models\rho\}
for a universal class
\mathcal{U}
. For a logic
L
, let
\Sigma(L)
be the least rule system containing
\{/\varphi:\varphi\in L\}
, that is,
\Sigma(L)=\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}+\{/\varphi:\varphi\in L\}
. Note that
\Sigma(L)=\mathcal{S}_{L}
. For a rule system
\mathcal{S}
, let
\Lambda(\mathcal{S})=\{\varphi:/\varphi\in\mathcal{S}\}
. Then,
\Sigma
and
\Lambda
preserve subset relation, and we have
\Lambda(\Sigma(L))=L
for a logic
L
and
\Sigma(\Lambda(\mathcal{S}))\subseteq\mathcal{S}
for a rule system
\mathcal{S}
. So, if a logic
L
is axiomatized by
\mathcal{R}
over
L_{0}
, then
L=\Sigma(\mathcal{S})
for a rule system
\mathcal{S}
axiomatized by
\mathcal{R}
over
\mathcal{S}_{L_{0}}
.

Stable canonical rules
are introduced in
[
7
]
as an alternative to canonical rules, the theory of which is developed in
[
22
]
. Both of them are generalizations of characteristic formulas to multi-conclusion rules. They are defined from finite modal algebras or finite modal spaces, and their validity has purely semantic characterizations. While canonical rules, generalizing Zakharyaschev’s canonical formulas
[
32
]
(see also
[
16
, Chapter 9]
), use selective filtration (see, e.g.,
[
16
, Section 5.5]
), stable canonical rules use the standard filtration (see, e.g.,
[
14
, Section 2.3]
and
[
16
, Section 5.3]
). A special feature of stable canonical rules is that, contrary to canonical rules, any rule can be axiomatized by stable canonical rules over the least normal modal rule system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
.

The basic idea of stable canonical rules as well as other characteristic formulas is very similar to
diagrams
widely used in model theory: to encode the structure of finite algebras or finite spaces (frames), but only partially.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a finite modal algebra and
D\subseteq A
. The
stable canonical rule

\rho(\mathfrak{A},D)
associated to
\mathfrak{A}
and
D
is the rule
\Gamma/\Delta
, where:

\displaystyle\Gamma=

\displaystyle\{p_{a\lor b}\leftrightarrow p_{a}\lor p_{b}:a,b\in A\}\cup

\displaystyle\{\lnot p_{a}\leftrightarrow\lnot p_{a}:a\in A\}\cup

\displaystyle\{\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}

\displaystyle\{p_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to4.62pt{\vbox to4.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.31197pt\lower-2.31197pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp_{a}:a\in D\},

and

\Delta=\{p_{a}:a\in A,a\neq 1\}.

Stable canonical rules can also be defined directly via finite modal spaces (i.e., finite Kripke frames).

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}=(X,R)
be a finite modal space and
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(X)
. Define the
stable canonical rule

\rho(\mathfrak{X},\mathcal{D})
associated to
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathcal{D}
as the rule
\Gamma/\Delta
, where:

\displaystyle\Gamma=

\displaystyle\{\bigvee\{p_{x}:x\in X\}\}\cup

\displaystyle\{p_{x}\to\lnot p_{y}:x,y\in X,x\neq y\}\cup

\displaystyle\{p_{x}\to\lnot\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp_{y}:x,y\in X,x\cancel{R}y\}\cup

\displaystyle\{p_{x}\to\bigvee\{\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp_{y}:y\in D\}:x\in X,D\in\mathcal{D},x\in R^{-1}[D]\},

and

\Delta=\{\lnot p_{x}:x\in X\}.

The following semantic characterization and axiomatization result are from
[
7
]
.

Theorem 2.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a finite modal algebra,
D\subseteq A
, and
\mathfrak{B}
be a modal algebra. Then

\mathfrak{B}\not\models\rho(\mathfrak{A},D)\text{ iff }\mathfrak{A}\hookrightarrow_{D}\mathfrak{B}.

For convenience, we provide the dual presentation of this fact. For a finite modal space
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(F)
, and a modal space
\mathfrak{X}
, we have

\mathfrak{X}\not\models\rho(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{D})\text{ iff }\mathfrak{X}\twoheadrightarrow_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{F}.

Theorem 2.8
.

1.

Every rule system
\mathcal{S}
is axiomatizable over
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
by stable canonical rules. Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}
is finitely axiomatizable over
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
, then
\mathcal{S}
is axiomatizable over
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
by finitely many stable canonical rules.

2.

Every logic
L
is axiomatizable over
\mathsf{K}
by stable canonical rules. Moreover, if
L
is finitely axiomatizable over
\mathsf{K}
, then
L
is axiomatizable over
\mathsf{K}
by finitely many stable canonical rules.

3
Definable filtrations and stable canonical rules

In this section, we identify definable filtration, a generalized type of filtration, as a key concept for developing the theory of stable canonical rules. We generalize the axiomatization result over
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
and
\mathsf{K}
(
[
7
]
) to any rule system or logic that admits definable filtration.

We assume familiarity with the filtration method widely used in modal logic. See, e.g.,
[
14
, Definition 2.36]
or
[
16
, Section 5.3]
for the standard filtration for Kripke frames and models, and
[
7
, Section 4]
for an algebraic account. Recall that in standard filtration, the equivalence relation
\sim_{\Theta}
is determined by the subformula-closed set
\Theta
. However, one can use a finer relation as long as the number of equivalence classes remains finite. As far as we know, this idea of generalized filtration appeared first in
[
29
]
. See also
[
1
]
for an overview and discussion of various notions of filtration.

For our purposes, even though we allow finer equivalence relations, we have to restrict ourselves to those induced by a set of formulas. This restriction ensures that the projection map from a modal space to its filtration is continuous and enables an algebraic presentation, which will be crucial for developing the theory of stable canonical rules and formulas in the subsequent sections. This leads to the concept of
definable filtration
, which was introduced in
[
24
]
for Kripke frames. Let us recall the definition of definable filtrations presented for modal spaces.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}=(X,R)
be a modal space,
V
be a valuation on
\mathfrak{X}
,
\Theta
be a finite subformula-closed set of formulas, and
\Theta^{\prime}
be a finite subformula-closed set of formulas containing
\Theta
. A
definable filtration of
(\mathfrak{X},V)
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
is a modal space
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},R^{\prime})
with a valuation
V^{\prime}
such that:

1.

X^{\prime}=X/{\sim_{\Theta^{\prime}}}
, where

x\sim_{\Theta^{\prime}}y\text{ iff }(\mathfrak{X},V,x\models\varphi\iff\mathfrak{X},V,y\models\varphi\text{ for all $\varphi\in\Theta^{\prime}$}),

2.

V^{\prime}(p)=\{[x]_{\Theta^{\prime}}:x\in V(p)\}
for
p\in\Theta^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}(p)=\emptyset
for
p\notin\Theta^{\prime}
,

3.

xRy
implies
[x]_{\Theta^{\prime}}R^{\prime}[y]_{\Theta^{\prime}}
,

4.

if
[x]_{\Theta^{\prime}}R^{\prime}[y]_{\Theta^{\prime}}
then (
y\models\varphi
implies
x\models\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi
for
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\in\Theta
).

We also call
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
a
definable filtration of
\mathfrak{X}
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
.

We drop the subscript
\Theta^{\prime}
when it is clear from the context. Note that the extended set
\Theta^{\prime}
of formulas is used to construct the quotient
X^{\prime}
, while the fourth requirement on the relation
R^{\prime}
is determined by
\Theta
. A standard filtration through is always a definable filtration by taking
\Theta^{\prime}=\Theta
.

Each equivalence class of
\sim_{\Theta^{\prime}}
can be represented as

\bigcap\{V(\varphi)^{*}:\varphi\in\Theta^{\prime}\},

where each
V(\varphi)^{*}
is either
V(\varphi)
or
V(\varphi)^{c}
, and thus is clopen in
\mathfrak{X}
. It follows that the projection map
X\twoheadrightarrow X/\sim_{\Theta^{\prime}}
is continuous. This allows an algebraic definition of definable filtrations.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=(A,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
be a modal algebra,
V
be a valuation on
\mathfrak{X}
,
\Theta
be a finite subformula-closed set of formulas, and
\Theta^{\prime}
be a finite subformula-closed set of formulas containing
\Theta
. A
definable filtration of
(\mathfrak{A},V)
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
is a modal algebra
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{\prime})
with a valuation
V^{\prime}
such that:

1.

A^{\prime}
is the Boolean subalgebra of
A
generated by
V[\Theta^{\prime}]\subseteq A
,

2.

V^{\prime}(p)=V(p)
for
p\in\Theta^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}(p)=0
for
p\notin\Theta^{\prime}
,

3.

The inclusion
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{A}
is a stable homomorphism satisfying CDC for
D
, where

D=\{V(\varphi):\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

We also call
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
a
definable filtration of
\mathfrak{A}
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
.

Note that the extended set
\Theta^{\prime}
of formulas is used to generate the Boolean subalgebra
A^{\prime}
, while the closed domain
D
is determined by
\Theta
. It is straightforward to verify that the two definitions of definable filtrations are dual to each other in the following sense, generalizing the proof for standard filtrations in
[
7
, Theorem 4.2]
. We will mostly use algebraic filtrations throughout the paper, while one can always reformulate it using the language of modal spaces.

.

. For a modal operator

The importance of the filtration method is summarized in the following lemma. Note that the statement may not hold for a formula in
\Theta^{\prime}{\setminus}\Theta
. See
Remark

4.2
for more discussion on the “mismatch” of
\Theta^{\prime}
and
\Theta
in the two definitions of definable filtrations.

Lemma 3.5

(Filtration Lemma)
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A}^{\prime},V^{\prime})
be a definable filtration of
(\mathfrak{A},V)
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
. Then
V(\varphi)=V^{\prime}(\varphi)
for all
\varphi\in\Theta
.

Proof.

By a routine induction on the complexity of
\varphi
. Use the fact that
\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{\prime}
for the base case, that
A^{\prime}
is a Boolean subalgebra of
A
for the Boolean cases, and that the inclusion is stable and satisfies CDC for
D
for the
case.
∎

Using the Filtration Lemma, we can automatically deduce the finite model property (fmp) for logics and rule systems that
admit definable filtration
.

Definition 3.6
.

1.

A class
\mathcal{C}
of modal algebras
admits definable filtration
if for any finite subformula-closed set
\Theta
of formulas, there is a finite subformula-closed set
\Theta^{\prime}
containing
\Theta
such that, for any modal algebra
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{C}
and any valuation
V
on
\mathfrak{A}
, there is a definable filtration
(\mathfrak{A}^{\prime},V^{\prime})
of
(\mathfrak{A},V)
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
.

2.

A modal logic
L

admits definable filtration
if the variety
\mathcal{V}(L)
admits definable filtration.

3.

A rule system
\mathcal{S}

admits definable filtration
if the universal class
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{S})
admits definable filtration.

Note that a logic
L
admits definable filtration iff the rule system
\mathcal{S}_{L}
admits definable filtration because they correspond to the same class of modal algebras.

Remark 3.7
.

The notion of admitting filtration has been used in the literature with various meanings and strengths. An explicit definition of
admitting filtration in the weak sense
and
admitting filtration in the strong sense
is provided in
[
8
]
, where the relation of the two definitions is also discussed. A similar discussion with Kripke frames and models can be found in
[
24
]
. We define it as
Definition

3.6
because this is just enough to prove
Theorem

3.9
and
Theorem

3.10
, though it is stronger than what is needed to prove
Proposition

3.8
.

Proposition 3.8
.

If a logic
L
admits definable filtration, then it has the fmp. If a rule system
\mathcal{S}
admits definable filtration, then it has the fmp.

Proof.

We only show the statement for rule systems, and the case for logics follows similarly. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a rule system that admits definable filtration. For any rule
\rho=\Gamma/\Delta\notin\mathcal{S}
, there is an
\mathcal{S}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{A}\not\models\rho
, witnessed by some valuation
V
on
\mathfrak{A}
. By the assumption that
\mathcal{S}
admits definable filtration, since
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{S})
, there is a finite subformula-closed set
\Theta^{\prime}
containing
\mathsf{Sub}(\Gamma\cup\Delta)
and a definable filtration
(\mathfrak{A}^{\prime},V^{\prime})
of
(\mathfrak{A},V)
for
\mathsf{Sub}(\Gamma\cup\Delta)
through
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{S})
. By
Lemma

3.5
,
\mathfrak{A},V\not\models\Gamma/\Delta
implies
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime},V^{\prime}\not\models\Gamma/\Delta
. Thus,
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
is a finite
\mathcal{S}
-algebra that refutes
\rho
. Hence,
\mathcal{S}
has the fmp.
∎

Many logics are known to admit standard filtration and thus admit definable filtration. For example,
\mathsf{K}
,
\mathsf{T}
, and
\mathsf{D}
admit the least and the greatest filtration. As for transitive logics,
\mathsf{K4}
and
\mathsf{S4}
admit the Lemmon filtration.

Now we prove the main theorems of this section, generalizing
Theorem

2.8
by extending the base rule system (or logic) from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
(or
\mathsf{K}
) to any one that admits definable filtration. The proof of
Theorem

2.8
uses standard filtration for
\mathsf{K}
to construct finite refutation patterns, which can then be represented by stable canonical rules. However, it is not the standard filtration itself but the existence of an appropriate algebraization (since the projection map to a filtrated space is continuous) that plays an important role in the proof. Thus, we can identify admitting definable filtration as a key feature of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathsf{K}}
that was used in the original proofs, allowing us to generalize the results. The proofs presented here are essentially the same as the original ones.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a rule system that admits definable filtration. For any rule
\rho
, there exist stable canonical rules
\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{1},D_{1}),\dots,\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{n},D_{n})
where each
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
is a finite
\mathcal{S}
-algebra and
D_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
, such that for any
\mathcal{S}
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,

\mathfrak{B}\models\rho\text{ iff }\mathfrak{B}\models\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{1},D_{1}),\dots,\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{n},D_{n}),

Proof.

Let
\rho=\Gamma/\Delta
be a rule. If
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{L}
, then we take
n=0
. Assume that
\rho\notin\mathcal{S}_{L}
and let
\Theta=\mathsf{Sub}(\Gamma\cup\Delta)
. By the assumption that
\mathcal{S}
admits definable filtration, there is a finite subformula-closed set
\Theta^{\prime}
containing
\Theta
such that, for any
\mathcal{S}
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}
and any valuation
V
on
\mathfrak{B}
, there is a definable filtration
(\mathfrak{B}^{\prime},V^{\prime})
of
(\mathfrak{B},V)
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\models\mathcal{S}
. Let
m=|\Theta^{\prime}|
. Since Boolean algebras are locally finite, up to isomorphism, there are finitely many tuples
(\mathfrak{A},V,D)
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

\mathfrak{A}
is a finite
\mathcal{S}
-algebra based on an at most
m
-generated Boolean algebra and
\mathfrak{A}\not\models\rho
,

2.

V
is a valuation on
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{A},V\not\models\rho
and
V(p)=0
for
p\notin\Theta^{\prime}
,

3.

D=\{V(\psi):\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\psi\in\Theta\}
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A}_{1},V_{1},D_{1}),\dots,(\mathfrak{A}_{n},V_{n},D_{n})
be an enumaration of such tuples. We show that for any
\mathcal{S}
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\rho
iff
\mathfrak{B}\models\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{1},D_{1}),\dots,\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{n},D_{n})
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{i},D_{i})
for some
1\leq i\leq n
. Then there is a stable embedding
h:\mathfrak{A}_{i}\hookrightarrow_{D_{i}}\mathfrak{B}
. Define a valuation
V
on
\mathfrak{B}
by
V(p)=h\circ V_{i}(p)
. Since
h
satisfies CDC for
D_{i}
, we have
V(\varphi)=h\circ V_{i}(\varphi)
for all
\varphi\in\Theta
. Therefore, since
V_{i}(\gamma)=1
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
V_{i}(\delta)\neq 1
for all
\delta\in\Delta
, it follows that
V(\gamma)=1
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
V(\delta)\neq 1
for all
\delta\in\Delta
, namely,
\mathfrak{B},V\not\models\rho
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\rho
. Let
V
be a valuation on
\mathfrak{B}
such that
\mathfrak{B},V\not\models\rho
and
V(p)=0
for
p\notin\Theta^{\prime}
. Then, there is a definable filtration
(\mathfrak{B}^{\prime},V^{\prime})
of
(\mathfrak{B},V)
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\models\mathcal{S}
. By the definition of definable filtrations,
B^{\prime}
is a Boolean subalgebra of
B
generated by
V[\Theta^{\prime}]
, so
B^{\prime}
as a Boolean algebra is at most
m
-generated. By
Lemma

3.5
, since
\Gamma\cup\Delta\subseteq\Theta
and
\mathfrak{B},V\not\models\rho
, we obtain
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime},V^{\prime}\not\models\rho
. Let
D=\{V(\psi):\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\psi\in\Theta\}
. Then, the tuple
(\mathfrak{B}^{\prime},V^{\prime},D)
is identical to
(\mathfrak{A}_{i},V_{i},D_{i})
for some
1\leq i\leq n
. Since
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow_{D}\mathfrak{B}
by the definition of definable filtration, we have
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\hookrightarrow_{D_{i}}\mathfrak{B}
, namely,
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{i},D_{i})
. Therefore, we conclude that
\mathfrak{B}\models\rho
iff
\mathfrak{B}\models\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{1},D_{1}),\dots,\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{n},D_{n})
.
∎

It follows directly from
Theorem

3.9
that any formula
\varphi
is also semantically equivalent to finitely many stable canonical rules over any rule system that admits definable filtration, by identifying
\varphi
with the rule
/\varphi
. Thus, we obtain the following axiomatization result.

Theorem 3.10
.

1.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a rule system that admits definable filtration. Every rule system
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{S}
is axiomatizable over
\mathcal{S}
by stable canonical rules. Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is finitely axiomatizable over
\mathcal{S}
, then
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is axiomatizable over
\mathcal{S}
by finitely many stable canonical rules.

2.

Let
L
be a logic that admits definable filtration. Every logic
L^{\prime}\supseteq L
is axiomatizable over
L
by stable canonical rules. Moreover, if
L^{\prime}
is finitely axiomatizable over
L
, then
L^{\prime}
is axiomatizable over
L
by finitely many stable canonical rules.

Proof.

1.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a rule system that admits definable filtration. For any rule system
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}+\{\rho_{i}:i\in I\}
for a set
\{\rho_{i}:i\in I\}
of rules. By
Theorem

3.9
, each rule
\rho_{i}
is semantically equivalent to a finite set of stable canonical rules
\{\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{ij},D_{ij}):1\leq j\leq n_{i}\}
for
\mathcal{S}
-algebras. So, for any
\mathcal{S}
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
iff
\mathfrak{B}\models\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{ij},D_{ij})
for all
i\in I
and
1\leq j\leq n_{i}
. Thus,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}+\{\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{ij},D_{ij}):i\in I,1\leq j\leq n_{i}\}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is finitely axiomatizable over
\mathcal{S}
, then we can choose
I
to be finite, hence the set
\{\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{ij},D_{ij}):i\in I,1\leq j\leq n_{i}\}
is also finite.

2.

Let
L
be a logic that admits definable filtration. For any logic
L^{\prime}\supseteq L
,
L^{\prime}=L+\{\varphi_{i}:i\in I\}
for a set
\{\varphi_{i}:i\in I\}
of formulas. So,
\Sigma(L^{\prime})=\Sigma(L)+\{/\varphi_{i}:i\in I\}
. By (1), there is a set
\{\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{j},D_{j}):j\in J\}
of stable canonical rules such that
\Sigma(L^{\prime})=\Sigma(L)+\{\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{j},D_{j}):j\in J\}
. Moreover, if
L^{\prime}
is finitely axiomatizable over
L
, then we can choose
I
to be finite, so that
J
can also be chosen to be finite by (1).

∎

As we mentioned, many logics, such as
\mathsf{K}
,
\mathsf{T}
,
\mathsf{D}
,
\mathsf{K4}
, and
\mathsf{S4}
admit standard filtration; we will see in the next section that pre-transitive logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}=\mathsf{K}+\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{m+1}p\to\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp
(
m\geq 1)
admit definable filtration (
Theorem

4.3
). Thus, these results apply to them and their corresponding rule systems.

Remark 3.11
.

If the base rule system
\mathcal{S}
is decidable, then the result of
Theorem

3.10
is computable; that is, given a finite axiomatization of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{S}
, a finite set of stable canonical rules that axiomatize
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{S}
can be computed. This can be shown by observing that if
\mathcal{S}
is decidable, then in the proof of
Theorem

3.9
, the enumeration of tuples
(\mathfrak{A},V,D)
is computable, so the finite set
\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{1},D_{1}),\dots,\rho(\mathfrak{A}_{n},D_{n})
of stable canonical rules that is equivalent to the given rule
\rho
is also computable. This holds as well if the base logic is decidable. Since a finitely axiomatizable rule system with the fmp is decidable, this is the case for all logics we mentioned above.

4
Gabbay’s filtration

In this section, we give an algebraic proof of the fact that a certain type of pre-transitive logics admits definable filtration. Recall that pre-transitive logics
\mathsf{K4^{m}_{n}}
are logics axomatized by
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{m}p\to\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{n}p
(or equivalently,
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{n}p\to\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{m}p
) over
\mathsf{K}
. The logic
\mathsf{K4^{m}_{n}}
defines the condition

\forall x\forall y\>(xR^{m}y\to xR^{n}y),

where
R^{k}
is the
k
-time composition of
R
, on modal spaces. Note that
\mathsf{K4^{2}_{1}}
is the transitive logic
\mathsf{K4}
.

We present a definable filtration construction for
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
(
m\geq 1
), meaning that when applied to a
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-algebra, the filtrated algebra is also a
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-algebra. This filtration will be used to develop the theory of stable canonical formulas for pre-transitive logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
in
Section

5
. The construction is the algebraic dual of the frame-theoretic filtration presented in the proof of
[
18
, Theorem 8]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=(A,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

.

\Theta^{\prime}
with
\Theta^{\prime\prime}=\mathsf{Sub}(\Theta^{\prime}\cup\{\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{m}\varphi:\varphi\in\Theta^{\prime}\})
. However, this would force us to extend the original set
\Theta
to an infinite set of formulas so that the filtrated algebra may no longer be finite. As far as we know, there is no standard filtration constructed for the logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

For any
m\geq 1
, the logic
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
admits definable filtration.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

4.1
. Note that
\Theta^{\prime}
defined in
Lemma

4.1
does not depend on
\mathfrak{A}
.
∎

Corollary 4.4

(
[
18
]
)
.

For any
m\geq 1
, the logic
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
has the fmp.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

4.3
and
Proposition

3.8
.
∎

5
Stable canonical formulas for pre-transitive logics

One of the main reasons that we had to move to rules when defining stable canonical rules is that there is a hidden universal quantifier in the semantical interpretation of rules. This allows stable canonical rules to have better control over the structure of modal algebras and enables us to prove the semantic characterization
Theorem

2.7
. However, if the
master modality
(see, e.g.,
[
14
, Section 6.5]
) is “definable” in the base logic, then we can turn stable canonical rules into formulas. This idea is realized in
[
7
, Section 6]
for
\mathsf{K4}
, where the theory of
stable canonical formulas
for
\mathsf{K4}
is developed as an alternative to Zakharyaschev’s canonical formulas
[
32
]
.

In this section, we develop the theory of stable canonical formulas for pre-transitive logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
, generalizing the stable canonical formulas for
\mathsf{K4}
. We already showed that these logics admit definable filtration (
Theorem

4.3
). As we can observe in
Definition

5.3
, the master modality is also definable in
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
in a similar manner as in
\mathsf{K4}
. As an application, we obtain a characterization of splittings and union-splittings in the lattice
\mathsf{NExt}{\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}}
via stable canonical formulas.

As we are dealing with logics in this section, it is useful to work with s.i. modal algebras. We recall the following lemma from
[
3
, Lemma 6.4]
, which is proved as a corollary of Venema’s characterization
[
31
]
of s.i. modal algebras.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a finite modal algebra and
\mathfrak{B}
be a s.i. modal algebra. If
\mathfrak{A}
is a stable subalgebra of
\mathfrak{B}
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{A}\hookrightarrow_{\emptyset}\mathfrak{B}
, then
\mathfrak{A}
is also s.i.

We first show how to construct finite refutation patterns for modal formulas. The proof is similar to
Theorem

3.9
.

Lemma 5.2
.

For any formula
\varphi
, there exist pairs
(\mathfrak{A}_{1},D_{1}),\dots,(\mathfrak{A}_{n},D_{n})
such that each
\mathfrak{A}_{i}=(A_{i},\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt_{i})
is a finite s.i.
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-algebra,
D_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
, and for any s.i. modal algebra
\mathfrak{B}=(B,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathfrak{B}\not\models\varphi
.

(2)

There is
1\leq i\leq n
and a stable embedding
h:\mathfrak{A}_{i}\hookrightarrow_{D_{i}}\mathfrak{B}
.

(3)

There is a s.i. homomorphic image
\mathcal{C}=(C,\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt)
of
\mathfrak{B}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and a stable embedding
h:\mathfrak{A}_{i}\hookrightarrow_{D_{i}}\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

If
\varphi\in\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
, then let
n=0
. Assume that
\varphi\notin\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
and let
\Theta=\mathsf{Sub}(\varphi)
. Since
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
admits definable filtration by
Theorem

4.3
, there is a finite subformula-closed set
\Theta^{\prime}
containing
\Theta
such that, for any
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-algebra
\mathfrak{B}
and any valuation
V
on
\mathfrak{B}
, there is a definable filtration
(\mathfrak{B}^{\prime},V^{\prime})
of
(\mathfrak{B},V)
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\models\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
. Let
m=|\Theta^{\prime}|
. Since Boolean algebras are locally finite, up to isomorphism, there are finitely many tuples
(\mathfrak{A},V,D)
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

\mathfrak{A}
is a finite s.i.
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-algebra based on an at most
m
-generated Boolean algebra and
\mathfrak{A}\not\models\varphi
,

2.

V
is a valuation on
\mathfrak{A}
such that
\mathfrak{A},V\not\models\varphi
and
V(p)=0
for
p\notin\Theta^{\prime}
,

3.

D=\{V(\psi):\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\psi\in\Theta\}
.

Let
(\mathfrak{A}_{1},V_{1},D_{1}),\dots,(\mathfrak{A}_{n},V_{n},D_{n})
be an enumaration of such tuples. We show that
(\mathfrak{A}_{1},D_{1}),\dots,(\mathfrak{A}_{n},D_{n})
is the desired pairs. Let
\mathfrak{B}
be a s.i.
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-algebra.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\varphi
. Let
V
be a valuation on
\mathfrak{B}
such that
\mathfrak{B},V\not\models\varphi
. Then there is a definable filtration
(\mathfrak{B}^{\prime},V^{\prime})
of
(B,V)
for
\Theta
through
\Theta^{\prime}
such that
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\models\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
. By the definition of definable filtration,
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
is a stable subalgebra of
\mathfrak{B}
, so
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}
is also s.i. by
Lemma

5.1
. Then the same argument as in the proof of
Theorem

3.9
shows that the tuple
(\mathfrak{B}^{\prime},V^{\prime},D)
is identical to
(\mathfrak{A}_{i},V_{i},D_{i})
for some
1\leq i\leq n
. Since
\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow_{D}\mathfrak{B}
by the definition of definable filtration, we conclude
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\hookrightarrow_{D_{i}}\mathfrak{B}
.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
. This is obvious by taking
\mathcal{C}=\mathfrak{B}
.

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
. Suppose that there is a s.i. homomorphic image
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathfrak{B}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and a stable embedding
h:\mathfrak{A}_{i}\hookrightarrow_{D_{i}}\mathcal{C}
. Let
V_{i}
be valuation on
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
such that
\mathfrak{A}_{i},V_{i}\not\models\varphi
. Define a valuation
V
on
\mathcal{C}
by
V(p)=h(V_{i}(p))
. The same argument as in the proof of
Theorem

3.9
shows that
\mathcal{C}\not\models\varphi
. Thus,
\mathfrak{B}\not\models\varphi
since
\mathcal{C}
is a homomorphic image of
\mathfrak{B}
.

∎

Now we define
stable canonical formulas
for pre-transitive logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
, which capture the semantic condition (3) in the theorem above.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a finite s.i.
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
-algebra and
D\subseteq A
. Let
\rho(\mathfrak{A},D)=\Gamma/\Delta
be the stable canonical rule defined in
Definition

2.5
. We define the
stable canonical formula

\gamma^{m}(\mathfrak{A},D)
as

\displaystyle\gamma^{m}(A,D)

\displaystyle=\bigwedge\{\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{\leq m}\gamma:\gamma\in\Gamma\}\to\bigvee\{\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{\leq m}\delta:\delta\in\Delta\}.

We will write
\gamma(\mathfrak{A},D)
for
\gamma^{1}(\mathfrak{A},D)
; this notation is consistent with
[
7
]
. The following lemma is a straightforward generalization of
[
3
, Lemma 4.1]
to pre-transitive logics
\mathsf{K4^{m+1}_{1}}
. Recall that a filter
F
on a modal algebra is a
-filter
if

thus

. So,
F
is a
-filter. By Zorn’s Lemma, there is a
-filter

such that
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
